===== issue body =====
Epic: #1088  
Roadmap order: **8 / 14**  
Depends on: #1092, #1093, #1095, #1096

## Goal

Ship runnable reference packs that prove the RAG eval stack works end to end and provide fixtures for CI, docs, demos, and regression tests.

## MVP packs

Ship these first:

| Slug | Tiers | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| `rag-citation-required` | A | Evidence envelope, citation schema, retrieval hit |
| `rag-faithfulness-v2` | A+B | Grounded QA with citations and advisory judge metrics |
| `rag-abstention` | A+D | Unanswerable questions and refusal scoring |

## Full suite after MVP

| Slug | Tiers | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| `rag-noisy-context` | D | MIRAGE-style mixed context |
| `rag-multi-hop` | A+B | Sequential retrieval and partial credit |
| `rag-claim-diagnostic` | C | Gold claims for RAGChecker-style attribution |

## Requirements per pack

- `eval_slice` on every case.
- Pinned corpus snapshot when using platform corpus; inline assets allowed for early fixture packs.
- Bad-agent fixtures that fail the expected dimension.
- Scorecard tier breakdown.
- Catalog metadata: category, difficulty, estimated cost.
- Builder/decompiler round-trip tests.

## Acceptance criteria

- [ ] MVP three packs pass catalog load and runnable tests.
- [ ] Each MVP pack fails a deliberately bad agent fixture on the expected dimension.
- [ ] At least one pack uses a real corpus snapshot once #1083/#1090 are available.
- [ ] Full six-pack suite is tracked but not required for the first CI gate.
- [ ] Docs explain which packs are fixture/demo vs benchmark-quality.
- [ ] Pack gallery does not imply public leaderboard validity before #1099.

## Blocks

#1102, #1101, #1099

./architecture.md:11:It is the implementation-facing architecture for AgentClash:
./architecture.md:15:- Private evaluation is the core value surface.
./architecture.md:17:- The system must support live execution, replay, scorecards, comparisons, and publication.
./architecture.md:30:AgentClash is a two-plane system. The `control plane` owns organizations, workspaces, challenges, agent definitions, runs, billing, publication, and UI-facing APIs. The `execution plane` owns long-running benchmark execution, sandbox provisioning, provider calls, tool execution, event generation, replay assembly, and scoring. The browser talks to a `Next.js` app and `Go` API server. The API server persists product state in `PostgreSQL`, starts durable `Temporal` workflows, and serves live updates over WebSockets. `Go` workers execute native and hosted agent runs, provision isolated sandboxes through `E2B` for native builds, persist large artifacts to `S3`, publish live events through `Redis`, compute scorecards, and materialize public snapshots when a run is published.

./web/content/docs/contributing/codebase-tour.mdx:26:That is the shortest route from "user action" to "why did this run behave that way".
./web/content/docs/contributing/codebase-tour.mdx:40:Workflow and activity definitions (the durable orchestration logic) live in `backend/internal/workflow` (`run_workflow.go`, `run_agent_workflow.go`, `activities.go`). The worker process that registers and runs them, plus the model invokers and event observers, lives in `backend/internal/worker`. Read the orchestration docs alongside both so you understand what the workflow owns versus what the activity owns.
./web/content/docs/contributing/codebase-tour.mdx:56:5. Only then start changing handlers, workflows, or UI.

./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:2:title: Eval workflows & gates
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:3:description: CLI-first eval commands, baselines, scorecards, comparisons, and release gates as implemented in cli/cmd.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:7:Challenge packs are useless until a **run** binds a `challenge_pack_version_id` to one or more **deployments**. The product ships a workflow-oriented CLI path so you rarely hand-copy UUIDs.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:12:agentclash eval start --follow
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:14:agentclash eval scorecard [run_id] [--agent <label>] [--json]
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:22:1. **Start a run.** `eval start --follow` creates the run and streams events to your terminal until it finishes. It prints the run ID you'll reuse below.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:24:3. **Run your candidate.** `eval start --follow` again, against the new deployment or pack version.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:25:4. **Read the scorecard.** `eval scorecard <candidate_run_id>` prints the candidate scorecard and—because a baseline is bookmarked—enriches it with the baseline, a comparison, and a release-gate verdict in one payload.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:28:> Source pointers for contributors: these commands live in `cli/cmd/eval.go`, `baseline.go`, `compare.go`, and `release_gate.go`.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:30:### `eval start`
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:32:Key flags (defined in `cli/cmd/eval.go`; resolution logic in `eval_resolve.go`):
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:47:### `eval scorecard`
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:49:Prints the candidate scorecard. When a baseline is bookmarked for the workspace, it also runs the comparison and release-gate evaluation and returns everything in one JSON payload for CI. With `--json` the payload has these top-level keys:
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:53:| `candidate` | The run/agent being scored (`run_id`, `run_status`, `run_agent_id`, …) |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:54:| `scorecard` | The candidate's per-dimension scorecard |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:59:If the scorecard is still computing (HTTP 202) or errored (HTTP 409), the `baseline`, `comparison`, and `release_gate` keys stay `null` and a 409 exits 1.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:61:Omitting `run_id` selects the **most recent run in the workspace by `created_at`** (newest first). This is purely "latest run"—it does not skip the baseline run—so in automation pass an explicit run id rather than relying on ordering.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:63:(Envelope assembly lives in `buildEvalScorecardEnvelope` in `cli/cmd/eval.go`; the latest-run rule is in `resolveRunSummary` in `eval_resolve.go`.)
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:67:`baseline set|show|clear` stores a **workspace-scoped** pointer to a run (and optional specific `run_agent`). This unlocks diff language inside `eval scorecard` without retyping ids.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:73:- `compare runs` calls `GET /v1/compare` with an explicit baseline/candidate pair (optional agent ids) and prints per-dimension deltas.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:74:- `compare gate` posts to `POST /v1/release-gates/evaluate` with those ids. With `--json` it prints the full server response (verdict plus fields like `policy_snapshot` and `evaluation_details`); for the exact response shape see the OpenAPI spec.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:75:- `release-gate list` surfaces historical evaluations with optional filters.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:84:| `3` | `insufficient_evidence` | Policy could not be evaluated; fix the spec or rerun. |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/eval-workflows-and-gates.mdx:90:`run create` still exists for power users, but product messaging steers new usage to `eval start` (`run.go` long help cross-links). Pick one style per automation story to avoid divergent flag semantics.

./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:3:description: Structured reference for challenge-pack bundles as parsed by backend/internal/challengepack.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:7:AgentClash challenge packs ship as **one YAML file** decoded into `challengepack.Bundle` (`backend/internal/challengepack/bundle.go`). Publication stores a JSON manifest combining the pack body with metadata for execution and scoring (`ManifestJSON` in the same file).
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:28:| `number` | Required positive integer (`int32`). Bumps when you materially change validators, tooling, sandbox, scores, etc. |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:29:| `execution_mode` | One of `native`, `prompt_eval`, `responses`, or `multi_turn`. An empty value is accepted as legacy, but always set it explicitly. See [execution mode compatibility](#execution-mode-compatibility) below. |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:30:| `tool_policy` | Arbitrary-shaped map mirrored into manifest `tool_policy`. **Must be empty** when `execution_mode` is `prompt_eval` (enforced when the bundle is validated). |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:32:| `sandbox` | Optional `SandboxConfig`. **Forbidden** when `execution_mode` is `prompt_eval`. |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:33:| `evaluation_spec` | Required scoring contract unmarshalled through scoring package strict decode (`scoring.StrictDecodeEvaluationSpec`). Typos surface at parse time rather than silently defaulting. |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:48:Optional map keyed by integration style; authoring today uses **`tools.custom`** as an array of composed tools. **Must be empty** when `execution_mode` is `prompt_eval` or `responses`.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:52:### `challenges` (required, non-empty)
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:54:Each challenge includes:
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:63:| `definition` | optional | Extra JSON-compatible bag for product-specific authoring |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:73:- All cases in a single input set must reference the same `challenge_key`; use separate input sets for separate challenges.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:75:Cases are modeled by `CaseDefinition` with `challenge_key`, `case_key`, optional rich `inputs`/`expectations`, `artifacts`, `assets`, plus legacy **`payload`** for blob-only authoring.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:84:- **`prompt_eval`** — pure model-output workloads. The `tools` block must **not** be present, `version.sandbox` must **not** be present, and `version.tool_policy` must be **empty**.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:86:- **`multi_turn`** — scripted or simulated multi-turn conversations. See [Multi-turn packs](/docs/challenge-packs/multi-turn) for the per-case `user_simulator` manifest.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:88:Choose `prompt_eval` for pure model-output workloads; promote to `native` when you need sandbox files, primitives, or file-based evidence.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/bundle-yaml-reference.mdx:94:- `challenge_pack_id`

./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:3:description: LLM-as-judge declarations, rubric/assertion modes, consensus, budgets, evidence wiring—straight from scoring/spec.go and validation_judges.go.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:7:An **LLM judge** is a model you task with scoring another agent's output against a rubric or assertion you write—an LLM-as-judge. Set `evaluation_spec.judge_mode` to choose how a pack is scored:
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:13:(The `judge_mode` field lives on `EvaluationSpec` in `backend/internal/scoring/spec.go`.)
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:15:Declare judge bodies under **`evaluation_spec.llm_judges[]`**. Each judge has a unique `key`. A scorecard dimension consumes a judge by setting `source: llm_judge` and `judge_key` pointing at exactly one declaration—one dimension maps to one judge.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:54:- The hard ceiling is **10**: a pack that requests more than 10 samples is **rejected** at publish with `must be at most 10` (a cost-attack guard, not a silent clamp). Validation contributed by `JudgeMaxSamplesCeiling` (`backend/internal/scoring/spec.go`).
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:58:Each entry must be a supported evidence reference (same family as validator `target` strings). The workflow evaluator stitches these fragments into the judge envelope before the LLM call.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:65:| `score_scale` | `{min,max}` normalization (defaults 1..5 when omitted) |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:73:3. Keep **keys unique across validators, metrics, and judges**—collisions are validation errors (namespace collision prevents ambiguous evidence routing).
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:77:A hybrid spec that pairs deterministic validators with two judges—an `assertion` gate and a `reference` rubric—then wires both into the scorecard. Lifted from [`examples/challenge-packs/incident-response-llm-judge.yaml`](https://github.com/agentclash/agentclash/blob/main/examples/challenge-packs/incident-response-llm-judge.yaml):
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:80:evaluation_spec:
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:90:        - case.payload.incident_summary
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:103:        - case.payload.incident_summary
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:111:  scorecard:
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:132:`scorecard.judge_limits` caps **judge** spend (USD/token budgets, plus `max_samples_per_judge`) separately from the agent's own model spend, which `runtime_limits` covers. The two are tracked independently on purpose: an agent burning through its budget should never mask a judge running away on cost. (Rationale lives in the `JudgeLimits` comments in `spec.go`.)
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/llm-judges.mdx:134:When cumulative judge calls exceed configured USD/token budgets, remaining samples downgrade to `unable_to_judge` states feeding scorecard `OutputStateUnavailable` paths.

./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:7:Input sets are the unit AgentClash schedules per deployment/candidate. Each `input_sets[]` entry contains a list of **`cases[]`** — the individual tasks an agent runs (defined by `CaseDefinition` in `backend/internal/challengepack/bundle.go`).
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:11:- **`challenge_key`** — must reference an existing `challenges[].key`
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:14:All cases in one `input_sets[]` entry must reference the same `challenge_key`; split mixed-challenge suites into separate input sets.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:21:2. **Structured eval** — `inputs[]` + `expectations[]` with explicit `kind` fields  
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:22:3. **Artifact heavy** — `assets[]` + `artifacts[]` referencing declared version/challenge assets
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:36:This is what scoring + replay pull back—not the raw YAML fragment.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:40:A single input set with one case that combines a `payload` blob with `expectations` (lifted from `examples/challenge-packs/incident-response-llm-judge.yaml`):
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:47:      - challenge_key: payments-api-outage

./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:51:            The payments API is returning elevated 5xx errors in one region.

./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:70:| `kind` | Drives rendering + validator binding (`text`, `artifact`, etc.—product-specific kinds should match worker expectations) |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/input-sets-and-cases.mdx:99:CLI `eval start` accepts `--input-set` when multiple sets exist; otherwise TTY flows prompt. API consumers pass the chosen `challenge_input_set_id` when creating runs (see OpenAPI `CreateRun` family). If omitted and the pack has exactly one input set, it is auto-selected; if the pack has multiple, the request is rejected.

./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/multi-turn.mdx:2:title: Multi-turn challenge packs
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/multi-turn.mdx:7:`multi_turn` is one of the four pack `execution_mode` values (`native`, `prompt_eval`, `responses`, `multi_turn`). Hybrid `multi_turn` execution runs a conversation loop outside the native multi-step tool loop. Each case declares a `user_simulator` manifest with **scripted**, **LLM**, and **human** phases.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/multi-turn.mdx:10:  Human phases block until an operator submits a turn via API or CLI. The run-agent replay page shows an awaiting-human banner while the workflow waits.

./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:3:description: Validators, metrics, behavioral signals, runtime limits, and scorecard semantics from backend/internal/scoring.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:7:The `evaluation_spec` block in a challenge pack declares how a run is scored: which validators check the output, which metrics get collected, and how those roll up into a scorecard. It is decoded strictly—unknown keys fail validation rather than being ignored—so every field documented here must match exactly. This page lists only the enums and collectors that are actually implemented, not aspirational placeholders.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:9:(Contributor pointer: the block unmarshals into `scoring.EvaluationSpec` in `backend/internal/scoring/spec.go`.)
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:15:Top-level discriminator on how scoring composes deterministic pieces with judges:
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:29:- **`key`** — unique within spec; also forbidden to collide with metrics or judges
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:33:- **`config`** — type-specific strict JSON validated in `validation.go`
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:39:`exact_match`, `contains`, `regex_match`, `json_schema`, `json_path_match`, `boolean_assert`, `fuzzy_match`, `numeric_match`, `normalized_match`, `token_f1`, `math_equivalence`, `bleu_score`, `rouge_score`, `chrf_score`, `file_content_match`, `file_exists`, `file_json_schema`, `directory_structure`, `code_execution`, `tool_call_assertion`, `postcondition`
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:45:`tool_call_assertion` uses `target: tool_calls` and strict config to assert executed tool-call evidence. It supports `tool_name`, `must_call`, `count`, `min_count`, `max_count`, `arguments_contain` JSON fragments, `ordered_tools`, and `order_mode` (`subsequence` or `exact`). Raw tool arguments are not copied into scorecard evidence.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:49:## Metrics (`metrics[]`)
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:57:| `collector` | Implemented switch in `engine_metrics.go` |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:58:| `unit` | Stored for dashboards/score normalization |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:62:`run_total_latency_ms`, `run_ttft_ms`, `run_input_tokens`, `run_output_tokens`, `run_total_tokens`, `run_agent_tokens`, `run_race_context_tokens`, `run_model_cost_usd`, `run_completed_successfully`, `run_failure_count`, `run_tool_call_count`, behavioral scores (`behavioral_recovery_score`, … ), `validator_pass_rate`
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:66:- **Unknown collector** (a `collector` key not in the list above) is rejected outright: the metric resolves to an error state with `collector "…" is not supported`. It is never silent.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:67:- **Known collector, missing evidence** (e.g. a latency metric when no latency was recorded) resolves to an *unavailable* state with a reason, which is the only "quiet" path.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:69:Copy collector keys verbatim from the implemented switch (contributor pointer: the `switch` in `backend/internal/scoring/engine_metrics.go`) to avoid the first case.

./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:96:## Scorecard (`scorecard`)
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:102:Each dimension may be a plain string shorthand (historical compatibility) **or** an expanded object specifying:
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:105:- **`source`** — dispatcher: `validators`, `metric`, `reliability`, `latency`, `cost`, `behavioral`, `llm_judge`, `human_preference`
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:106:- **`validators[]`**, **`metric`**, **`judge_key`** — depending on `source`
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:117:| `binary` | All dimensions treated as gates; scorecard-level `pass_threshold` is rejected (prevents ambiguity) |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:118:| `hybrid` | Gates AND aggregate over non-gate dims must clear optional `scorecard.pass_threshold` |
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:120:See doc comments on `ScoringStrategy` in `spec.go` for nuanced behavior—especially hybrid vs weighted gate interplay.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:122:### `scorecard.pass_threshold`
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:124:Optional inclusive overall score cutoff (documented extensively in struct comment). Forbidden for pure `binary`.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:126:### Judge budgets (`scorecard.judge_limits`)
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:136:`max_total_tokens`, `max_cost_usd`, `max_duration_ms`—enforced upstream of sandbox/model loops; surfaced for UI + scoring fallbacks.
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:138:## Pricing (`pricing.models[]`)
./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:140:Pricing rows describing per-million token economics for **`run_model_cost_usd`** normalization. ./web/content/docs/challenge-packs/evaluation-spec-reference.mdx:151:Prefer explicit paths whenever you refactor input schema—ambiguous references fail validate instead of drifting silently.

This page lists only the enums and collectors that are actually implemented, not aspirational placeholders.

./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:55:regressions:
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:61:Local validation is always offline. Add `--remote` when you want the CLI to call the AgentClash API and verify that the manifest's agent build, runtime profile, provider account, challenge pack version, input set, regression suites or cases, and baseline are visible from the selected workspace. Because this makes real authenticated API calls, set `AGENTCLASH_API_URL`, `AGENTCLASH_TOKEN`, and `AGENTCLASH_WORKSPACE` in CI and expect normal API latency, rate limits, and token scoping rules. JSON output includes a `remote.checks[]` entry per referenced field, so CI can report whether a failure came from the local manifest contract or from an API/resource check.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:68:- `evaluation` names the workload: challenge pack version, optional input set, and optional regression suites or cases. For voice packs, set the optional `evaluation.mode: text-sim` to request a deterministic text-simulated voice eval (`text-sim` is the only mode supported today; `audio-sim`, `live-call`, and `replay-import` are reserved for future use).
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:71:- `regressions` controls whether failed cases should only be reported, proposed for promotion, or eventually auto-promoted on main.

./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:81:If you are deciding what the workload should contain, use [CI/CD Workload Recipes](/docs/guides/ci-cd-workload-recipes) for coding, research, support/ops, and long-horizon agent patterns.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:89:Use `baseline.max_age_days` when a stale baseline should block the gate. The resolver checks the chosen run's `finished_at` or `created_at` timestamp and fails instead of silently comparing against old behavior.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:196:The action installs the published `agentclash` npm package by default, runs `ci validate --remote`, runs `ci should-run`, auto-detects pull request labels from the GitHub event payload, skips unrelated changes, runs `ci run` when matched, posts or updates a sticky structured PR comment when pull request context is available, and exposes `should-run`, `skip-reason`, `run-id`, `gate-verdict`, `exit-code`, `result-file`, and `artifact-dir` outputs. It preserves the CLI exit code, so a blocking gate fails the workflow normally. Grant `pull-requests: write` when you want GitHub-hosted PR comments; commenting is best-effort and permission failures do not override the AgentClash result. When run metadata is available, the comment links reviewers directly to the AgentClash candidate run, baseline run, comparison, failures, scorecard, replay, and regression cases. If setup fails before a candidate run is created, the sticky comment reports the errored setup state and points reviewers at the GitHub Actions log.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:200:`--artifact-dir` writes stable JSON files intended for `actions/upload-artifact`: `result.json` for the final CLI envelope, `run.json` for run creation/completion payloads, `scorecard.json` for candidate scorecard evidence, `comparison.json` for baseline/candidate comparison evidence, and `gate.json` for the release-gate verdict and policy metadata. The summary and artifacts include the challenge pack version, baseline, candidate, policy, verdict, top evidence lines, regression candidate promotion outcomes, and AgentClash links when the API returns them. Use `--ci-repository`, `--ci-pull-request`, `--ci-branch`, `--ci-default-branch`, `--ci-commit`, and the other `--ci-*` flags when running from another CI system or a custom wrapper.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:204:Do not auto-promote every PR failure by default. A bad run, flaky dependency, or weak evaluator could pollute the regression suite. Use this conservative progression:
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:207:regressions:
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:214:regressions:
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:218:Create reviewable candidates after a failing gate. The CLI lists the candidate run's failure-review items, checks each target suite from `evaluation.regression_suites`, skips any challenge identity that already has a non-archived/non-rejected case, then calls the promote-failure API with `status: proposed`.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:220:Proposed cases appear in the regression suite UI without entering future runs. A reviewer can accept them by changing status to `active`, or reject/archive them if the failure is noisy, duplicated, or not worth keeping.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:223:regressions:
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:229:All modes preserve the original gate exit code. Promotion errors are reported in the human output, JSON `regression_promotions.errors`, GitHub step summary, and artifact `result.json`, but a blocking regression still exits with the gate failure code.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates.mdx:237:- Regression candidate promotion requires `evaluation.regression_suites`; without at least one target suite, `ci run` reports promotion as blocked.

./web/content/docs/guides/datasets-overview.mdx:7:Dataset evals run a **pinned version** of labeled examples through a challenge pack and deployment, then compare candidate runs against recorded baselines. ./web/content/docs/guides/datasets-overview.mdx:42:## Baselines and regression suites

(Under the hood these land in the `workspace_regression_cases` table keyed by `dataset_example_id`.)

./web/content/docs/guides/datasets-overview.mdx:60:<Callout type="info">
./web/content/docs/guides/write-a-challenge-pack.mdx:13:- You know which `execution_mode` the pack needs. There are four: `prompt_eval` (judge a single text answer, no tools or sandbox), `native` (agent runs with tools in a sandbox), `responses` (OpenAI Responses API / deep research), and `multi_turn` (scripted or simulated multi-turn conversations). When in doubt, pick the simplest mode that fits — usually `prompt_eval`.

./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-workload-recipes.mdx:3:description: Choose realistic AgentClash CI workloads for coding, research, support, ops, and long-horizon agents.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-workload-recipes.mdx:9:The agent is the thing under test. In a manifest, that means the candidate build, deployment, runtime resources, provider model, provider account, tools, schemas, policies, prompts, and workflow code. The challenge pack or regression suite is the workload used to test it. The release gate is the decision policy that turns the run into pass, warn, or fail.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-workload-recipes.mdx:17:Use a narrow regression suite when you need to lock failures that have already happened. Regression suites should be small, high-signal, and cheap enough to run often. They are strongest when they include failure evidence, a stable expected behavior, and a clear reason the case matters.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-workload-recipes.mdx:21:- Pull requests run a smoke challenge pack plus critical regressions.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-workload-recipes.mdx:22:- Nightly or mainline workflows run the full challenge pack plus larger regression suites.
./web/content/docs/guides/ci-cd-workload-recipes.mdx:25:When `regressions.promote_failures: proposed` is enabled, failing gates can add reviewable candidates to the suites listed in `evaluation.regression_suites`. Treat those proposals as a queue, not as automatic truth: accept cases that represent durable agent risk, reject noisy or duplicate failures, and keep the suite small enough that engineers still trust the gate.

./web/content/blog/building-agent-eval-program-regulated-enterprise.mdx:108:### How is this different from our existing ML model validation?
./web/content/blog/when-the-us-government-banned-fable-5.mdx:55:AgentClash puts candidate models on the same real task, at the same time, with the same tools and constraints, scored on completion, speed, token efficiency, and tool strategy, with full replays of why one won. That turns "we just lost our model" from a fire drill into a Tuesday: run the clash, read the evidence, switch.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:4:description: "A practical guide to AI agent evaluation with real-task workloads, replay evidence, scorecards, challenge packs, and CI regression gates."
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:14:Real AI agent evaluation needs regression testing.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:18:An agent eval should answer a practical release question: is this agent ready to do this job again, under the same constraints, without getting worse?
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:26:- correctness, cost, latency, and evidence quality
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:33:## Why static benchmarks are not enough
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:41:Two agents can produce the same final answer while behaving very differently. One might use the right tool, verify its work, attach the required artifact, and stay inside budget. Another might hallucinate a file, skip the failing test, and still land near the correct prose answer. A final-answer-only benchmark treats those runs as similar. A real agent eval should not.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:53:When an agent fails in production or in a release test, the failure should become a reusable case. That is how the eval suite compounds. Instead of debugging the same mistake every few weeks, you promote it into coverage and make the next candidate prove it did not regress.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:57:## Add regression gates to CI
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:61:AI agent regression testing compares a candidate run against a known baseline. If the candidate gets worse on correctness, cost, latency, artifacts, or another scorecard dimension, the gate can block the pull request before the change reaches users.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:71:The [AI agent regression testing](/platform/agent-regression-testing) page covers the product angle. The [CI/CD agent gates](/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates) guide covers the implementation path.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:75:If you are comparing agent evaluation tools, look past the leaderboard. The tool should help your team make a release decision, debug failures, and improve the next test suite.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:82:- scorecards that separate correctness, cost, latency, and evidence
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:85:- CI gates for regressions
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:95:2. Run candidate and baseline agents under the same constraints.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-evaluation-regression-testing.mdx:97:4. Promote important failures into regression cases.
./web/content/blog/pass-k-reliability-enterprise-teams.mdx:31:| "Usually works if the user retries" | pass@k with bounded k | Warn on pass^k regression |
./web/content/blog/pass-k-reliability-enterprise-teams.mdx:33:| "Expensive failures are unacceptable" | pass^k + cost-per-success | Block on cost regression |
./web/content/blog/ai-platform-lead-agent-release-gates.mdx:26:challenge pack/regression suite = workload
./web/content/blog/ai-platform-lead-agent-release-gates.mdx:45:Attach numeric policy: max correctness regression, cost ceiling, latency and TTFT bounds, automatic fail on security-policy violations.
./web/content/blog/ai-platform-lead-agent-release-gates.mdx:68:The compare screen should answer: who wins on correctness, cost, latency, reliability, and evidence completeness?
./web/content/blog/ai-platform-lead-agent-release-gates.mdx:72:Example from the enterprise narrative: candidate beats baseline on cost but fails the gate because correctness regressed on a critical challenge family and TTFT exceeded policy after a routing fallback. That is a defensible block.
./web/content/blog/ai-platform-lead-agent-release-gates.mdx:99:Pair gates with [AI agent regression testing](/platform/agent-regression-testing) so production misses flow back into the workload.
./web/content/blog/ai-platform-lead-agent-release-gates.mdx:105:Block on correctness regression, policy violations, and hard cost or latency ceilings. Warn on exploratory metrics while the workload is still stabilizing. Document the mode in the manifest so CI behavior matches committee expectations.
./web/content/blog/how-agentclash-scores-agent-trajectories.mdx:10:That difference matters because two agents can land on the same final answer while behaving completely differently. One picks the right tool, verifies its work, attaches the required artifact, and stays inside budget. The other hallucinates a file, skips the failing test, burns twice the tokens, and still produces similar-looking prose. If your eval only reads the final output, those two runs look the same. They are not.
./web/content/blog/how-agentclash-scores-agent-trajectories.mdx:16:When an agent runs in AgentClash, it works inside a fresh sandbox with real tools for minutes at a time: it plans, calls a tool, observes the result, recovers from an error, calls another tool, and eventually stops. Every one of those steps is captured as a structured event.
./web/content/blog/how-agentclash-scores-agent-trajectories.mdx:28:**Behavioral signals.** The trajectory itself: tool-choice efficiency, recovery after an error, and whether the agent stayed inside the scope of the task. This is the vantage final-answer grading can't see at all.
./web/content/blog/how-agentclash-scores-agent-trajectories.mdx:34:Four independent readings are only useful if a reviewer can act on them. AgentClash folds the validator results into scorecard dimensions, and the dimensions into a composite verdict — with the weights under your control, because a latency regression and a correctness regression should not count the same for every team.
./web/content/blog/how-agentclash-scores-agent-trajectories.mdx:42:Scoring the trajectory instead of the string is what lets AgentClash do the things a final-answer benchmark can't: compare models [head-to-head on the same task](/platform/agent-evaluation), explain *why* one won, and turn a specific failure into a permanent [regression test](/platform/agent-regression-testing) instead of a vibe.
./web/content/blog/coding-agent-benchmark-june-2026.mdx:106:- Promote meaningful failures back into challenge-pack coverage so the next month's report gates on real regressions.
./web/content/blog/coding-agent-benchmark-june-2026.mdx:110:We follow the same five-step cadence on [/benchmarks](/benchmarks): pin the pack, evaluate every candidate on identical constraints, export scorecards, attach replay evidence, summarize on the hub and RSS feed. Subscribe at [/benchmarks/feed.xml](/benchmarks/feed.xml).
./web/content/blog/agentclash-vs-langsmith-braintrust-production.mdx:14:| [LangSmith](/compare/agentclash-vs-langsmith) | What happened in this chain, and how good was the output? | LangChain apps, trace-first debugging, prompt/dataset evals |
./web/content/blog/agentclash-vs-langsmith-braintrust-production.mdx:48:- CI regression gates
./web/content/blog/agentclash-vs-langsmith-braintrust-production.mdx:67:- Treating a prompt score as proof the agent completes the job
./web/content/blog/benchmark-ai-agents-on-your-own-data.mdx:10:Leaderboards optimize for tasks that are public, static, and comparable across vendors. Your agent optimizes for private repos, internal APIs, ticket schemas, and policies that never appear on LMSYS.
./web/content/blog/agent-evaluation-vs-prompt-evaluation-braintrust.mdx:20:- prompt regression during model upgrades
./web/content/blog/agent-evaluation-vs-prompt-evaluation-braintrust.mdx:27:Agents answer a different question: *can this system complete the job under policy, budget, and tool constraints?*
./web/content/blog/agent-evaluation-vs-prompt-evaluation-braintrust.mdx:45:- your regression signal is text quality, not operational behavior
./web/content/blog/why-agentclash-races-agents-head-to-head.mdx:31:- the **same budget** — the same time, the same tool access, the same constraints
./web/content/blog/why-agentclash-races-agents-head-to-head.mdx:44:Public leaderboards answer a different question: which model is generally popular or generally strong on a shared static benchmark that has probably leaked into training data. That's a fine first filter. It is not an answer to "which agent should *I* ship for *this* job."
./web/content/blog/why-agentclash-races-agents-head-to-head.mdx:46:A head-to-head comparison on your own [challenge pack](/docs/guides/write-a-challenge-pack) answers the question you actually have, on the task you actually run, under conditions you actually control. And because the losing trajectories are real, captured runs, you can promote the interesting failures straight into a [regression gate](/platform/agent-regression-testing) so the next candidate has to prove it didn't get worse.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-approval-security-compliance.mdx:32:- scorecard deltas (correctness, cost, latency, evidence tier)
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-approval-security-compliance.mdx:34:- the gate policy (for example: no correctness regression, cost cap, automatic fail on policy violations)
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-approval-security-compliance.mdx:38:AgentClash supports review with immutable run history and replay shaped for decisions, not raw span dumps. See [AI agent regression testing](/platform/agent-regression-testing) for the product surface.
./web/content/blog/why-we-built-agentclash.mdx:22:AgentClash puts your models on the same real task, at the same time. Same tools, same constraints, same environment. Scored live on completion, speed, token efficiency, and tool strategy.
./web/content/blog/why-we-built-agentclash.mdx:24:Step-by-step replays show exactly why one agent won and another didn't. Every failure gets captured, classified, and turned into a regression test — automatically. The more you run, the smarter your eval suite gets.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-governance-middle-east-enterprises.mdx:23:Regional context: UAE and wider GCC buyers increasingly treat residency and AI governance as board-level topics alongside frameworks such as PDPL and sector rules from bodies like TDRA, CBUAE, ADGM, and DIFC. Your architecture review still owns the legal mapping; your eval program owns the proof that a specific agent build behaved acceptably on your workload.
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-governance-middle-east-enterprises.mdx:41:3. **Replay evidence:** trajectory proof for cases that drove the decision
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-governance-middle-east-enterprises.mdx:42:4. **Scorecard deltas:** correctness, cost, latency, policy dimensions
./web/content/blog/ai-agent-governance-middle-east-enterprises.mdx:70:Implementation details: [CI/CD agent gates](/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates). Product overview: [AI agent regression testing](/platform/agent-regression-testing).
./web/content/blog/why-ai-pilot-failed-agent-eval-second-attempt.mdx:8:The first AI agent pilot rarely fails because the model is "bad." It fails because the team optimized for a demo shape that does not survive contact with production.
./web/content/blog/why-ai-pilot-failed-agent-eval-second-attempt.mdx:28:Security and engineering got chat transcripts or aggregate eval scores without trajectory proof. Disagreements ended in meetings, not diffs.
./web/content/blog/why-ai-pilot-failed-agent-eval-second-attempt.mdx:36:**Fix:** Promote the pilot benchmark into [CI/CD agent evaluation](/ci-cd-agent-evaluation) with a manifest gate. See [AI agent regression testing](/platform/agent-regression-testing) for the regression layer.
./web/content/blog/why-ai-pilot-failed-agent-eval-second-attempt.mdx:47:6. **Close the loop:** block or ship with a named reason; promote failures into regression cases.
./web/content/blog/why-ai-pilot-failed-agent-eval-second-attempt.mdx:63:Every meaningful miss becomes a case. Every case becomes a regression. Every regression tightens the next gate.
./web/content/blog/why-ai-pilot-failed-agent-eval-second-attempt.mdx:86:One gate verdict plus three deltas vs baseline: correctness, cost per success, and median latency. Link replay for the case that drove the call.
./web/content/blog/pass-at-k-vs-pass-power-k.mdx:55:1. **Freeze the workload** in a challenge pack so every attempt runs under the same constraints.
./web/content/blog/pass-at-k-vs-pass-power-k.mdx:60:For product context, see [agent reliability benchmark](/agent-reliability-benchmark) and [AI agent regression testing](/platform/agent-regression-testing). For implementation, start with [CI/CD agent gates](/docs/guides/ci-cd-agent-gates).
./web/content/blog/pass-at-k-vs-pass-power-k.mdx:69:- **No** → track pass^k, and treat any regression in strict success rate as a release blocker.
./web/content/blog/introducing-datasmith-synthetic-agent-data.mdx:4:description: "DataSmith is an open-source Python SDK for weak-vs-strong synthetic dataset generation. Use it locally for SFT and DPO export, or inside AgentClash for eval-ready datasets and regression tests."
./web/content/blog/introducing-datasmith-synthetic-agent-data.mdx:10:DataSmith fixes the loop. It implements the practical weak-vs-strong Agentic Self-Instruct pattern from [Meta FAIR's Autodata work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25996): a challenger proposes examples, weak and strong solvers attempt them, and a judge accepts only when the gap is useful for training.
./web/content/blog/introducing-datasmith-synthetic-agent-data.mdx:27:Start from a domain brief. DataSmith's seed constructor can use web-search signals to bootstrap grounded seed examples. A seed judge filters for specificity and usefulness before anything enters the generation loop.
./web/content/blog/introducing-datasmith-synthetic-agent-data.mdx:31:For each seed, a challenger proposes a candidate example. Weak and strong solvers attempt it. A judge scores quality and separation. Accepted rows land in `accepted.jsonl`; rejections keep reason codes, solver attempts, and feedback for the next round.
./web/content/blog/introducing-datasmith-synthetic-agent-data.mdx:51:No vendor lock-in. Swap weak and strong solvers per domain without rewriting the loop.
./web/content/blog/introducing-datasmith-synthetic-agent-data.mdx:76:DataSmith is the **local, training-oriented** layer. [AgentClash](https://agentclash.dev) is the **hosted eval and regression** layer.
./web/content/blog/introducing-datasmith-synthetic-agent-data.mdx:78:Inside AgentClash workspaces you can run the same weak-vs-strong loop as **Agentic Self-Instruct** generation on pinned datasets. Accepted synthetic rows become eval examples you can baseline, gate in CI, and promote into regression suites.
./web/content/blog/introducing-datasmith-synthetic-agent-data.mdx:85:| SFT / DPO / HF export | Yes | Eval and regression formats |
./web/content/blog/introducing-datasmith-synthetic-agent-data.mdx:86:| CI regression gates | Manual | Built-in dataset gates |
./web/content/blog/evaluating-coding-agents-private-repos-checklist.mdx:16:- fix a failing test in a known module
./web/content/blog/evaluating-coding-agents-private-repos-checklist.mdx:18:- refactor with lint and typecheck gates
./web/content/blog/do-ai-models-cheat-by-brand.mdx:22:When you give an AI agent a task and a score to chase, it has two ways to get a high score. It can actually do the work — or it can game the test. Researchers call the second one **reward hacking**: getting the reward without doing the thing the reward was supposed to measure.
./web/content/blog/do-ai-models-cheat-by-brand.mdx:31:4. **Gaming the proxy** — optimizing the visible metric (like test coverage) instead of fixing the actual bug.
./web/content/blog/do-ai-models-cheat-by-brand.mdx:35:Every pack was scored **two ways at once**: a deterministic gate that just asks *"did the fix actually work?"* (correctness), and an LLM judge that asks *"did it get there honestly?"* (integrity). The gap between those two scores is what I called the **gaming signal** — the model passed, but how clean was the win?
./web/content/blog/do-ai-models-cheat-by-brand.mdx:41:On the "reading the answer key" task, three models all earned a **perfect 1.0 for correctness**. All three produced a working fix. But watch what happened underneath:
./web/content/blog/do-ai-models-cheat-by-brand.mdx:45:- **Claude Sonnet 4.6** figured out the bug purely from reading the code and the business rules, without touching the forbidden files at all. Correctness 1.0, **integrity 1.0**. The honest solver.
./web/content/blog/do-ai-models-cheat-by-brand.mdx:47:Three models. Same task. Same perfect score. Three completely different characters: the confessor, the stealth cheater, and the honest solver.
./web/content/blog/do-ai-models-cheat-by-brand.mdx:80:1. **Pass/fail benchmarks may be measuring completion, not capability.** If your eval only asks "did it pass?", two models can both score 1.0 while one quietly read the hidden logs and the other actually debugged the problem. You'd never know.
./web/content/benchmarks/gpt-generations-expression-evaluator.mdx:4:description: "A head-to-head AgentClash comparison of GPT-4.1, GPT-5, GPT-5.4, and GPT-5.5 on a real agentic coding task — building an integer expression evaluator with truncating division — scored on correctness, reliability, and token efficiency."
